## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Score decreases by 1 for items from one source half (good half), increases by 1 for items from the other half (bad half)
- Nest = WHITE floor, wall = RED
- Source = BLACK floor, split into BLUE half (one type) and GREEN half (other type)
- We want to minimize score, so we want to deliver items that decrease the score

**Analyzing the champion (score -12.39):**
- State 0: Exploration (random walk, rwm=19) with 3 transitions:
  - → State 1 with prob 0.8989 (random)
  - → State 1 with prob 0.4698 (random, duplicate destination)
  - → State 1 if neighbors > 8 (high threshold)
- State 1: GoAwayColor (clr=2=green), so moving AWAY from GREEN walls
  - → State 0 with prob 0.9692 (random, very likely to return)
  - → State 0 if floor is BLACK (prob 0.7499)
  - → State 0 if sees color 5 (magenta) with prob 0.8499

**Key insight:** GoAwayColor from GREEN (clr=2) means robots move toward the BLUE side. If BLUE side decreases score, this makes sense. The robot goes to the BLACK floor (source), picks up a BLUE item, then returns.

**Strategy:** The champion essentially does: explore → go away from green (toward blue source) → when on black floor, return to explore (and presumably navigate back toward nest via white floor).

**Better approach ideas:**
1. Explicitly use GoToColor toward BLUE (clr=3) and navigate back toward RED (nest wall) or WHITE floor
2. Use floor color detection more explicitly: BLACK = at source, WHITE = at nest
3. 3-state machines with clearer foraging loop

Let me design variations:

**Machine designs:**
1. **Champion (exact copy)** - survival
2. **Champion variant** - tweak probabilities slightly
3. **3-state explicit forager**: Explore → GoToColor(BLUE) when not at black → Return to RED when on black
4. **GoToColor BLUE + GoToColor RED**: Two-state, go to blue source then go back to red wall
5. **Explicit forager with floor detection**: Explore on gray, go to black (source), go away from green, go to white
6. Various other topologies

- Go to BLUE source (GoToColor clr=3 or GoAwayColor clr=2)

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 3 --clr0 3 --n0 3 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.9 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.05 --n0x2 0 --c0x2 7 --p0x2 0.8 --l0x2 3 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --cle1 1 --clr1 1 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.9 --n1x1 0 --c1x1 5 --p1x1 0.03

Machine 14: Champion with GoAwayGreen but different return condition (WHITE floor)
FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 19 --cle0 6 --n0 2 --n0x0 2 --c0x0 5 --p0x0 0.8 --n0x1 2 --c0x1 7 --p0x1 0.9 --l0x1 3 --s1 9 --vel1 1.0 --cle1 5 --clr1 2 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 0 --p1x0 0.8 --n1x1 0 --c1x1 2 --p1x1 0.9

Wait, `--n0x0 2` with 2 states from state 0 - D can only be 0. Let me fix.

For 2-state machines: from state 0, only D=0 (→ state 1); from state 1, only D=0 (→ state 0).

Let me redo machines 10-14 more carefully. The champion uses D=2 and D=3 but those seem to wrap or the simulator handles it. Since the champion works, I'll keep those patterns for champion variants, but for new 2-state machines I'll use D=0.

Actually wait - the champion has `--n0x0 0`, `--n0x1 2`, `--n0x2 2` for state 0 (2 states). With 2 states, from state 0, the only destination is state 1 (D=0). D=2 might just be treated as D=0 (modulo 1 = 0). So all 3 transitions from state 0 go to state 1. Similarly for state 1: `--n1x0 3`, `--n1x1 0`, `--n1x2 3` - D=3 mod 1 = 0, D=0 mod 1 = 0. All go to state 0. This makes sense - the machine effectively just has multiple conditions to switch between the two states.

OK so for 2-state machines I can use any D value and they all go to the other state. I'll use D=0 for clarity in my new machines.

Let me finalize

The champion goes away from GREEN walls → toward BLUE side → picks up blue items → returns when on black floor or randomly. This implies BLUE items decrease score (good).

  - Neighbors > 9 (steepness 12)
- State 1: GoAwayColor(green=clr2, magenta LED), 3 transitions all → state 0:
  - Random 0.97
  - Floor BLACK prob 0.75
  - Sees magenta (clr=5) prob 0.85

The robot explores, then flees green walls (moving toward blue side), picks up blue items, returns when on black floor or randomly.

**Key insight:** The champion is essentially: explore briefly → go away from green (toward blue source) → return when at source (black floor). The high random probabilities mean it oscillates rapidly between states, spending time in both.

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score -13.42):**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=22, cyan), 4 transitions → state 1:
  - Random 0.9
  - Random 0.45
  - Neighbors > 9, steepness 12
  - Floor WHITE prob 0.85
- State 1: GoAwayColor(green=clr2, magenta), 3 transitions → state 0:
  - Random 0.97
  - Floor BLACK prob 0.75
  - Sees magenta (clr=5) prob 0.85

The added WHITE floor transition (go to GoAwayGreen when at nest) is the improvement over the previous champion. This makes sense: when at the white nest area, switch to fleeing green = heading toward blue source.

**Key insights:**
- Going away from GREEN walls moves robots toward BLUE source
- WHITE floor = nest (deliver items here)
- BLACK floor = source (pick up items)
- The robot: explores → when at white floor (nest), flee green (go to source) → when at black (source), return to explore
- The random transitions create oscillation that helps navigate

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Tweak probabilities around champion values (±10%)
2. Try different steepness values for neighbor condition
3. Try adding more conditions to state 1
4. Try adding BLACK floor condition to state 0 (go to GoAwayGreen when at source too)
5. Try swapping some random transitions for floor-based ones

**Exploration:**
- 3-state machines with explicit nest return
- Try GoToColor(blue) instead of GoAwayColor(green)
- Try adding neighbor-below condition

**What works:** Higher WHITE floor probability (0.95) triggers GoAwayGreen more reliably at nest. BLACK floor triggers return to explore. The magenta camera condition sees other robots in state 1 (also running GoAwayGreen).

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Try p0x3 even higher (0.98, 1.0)
2. Try different combinations of the 4th transition (black floor instead of white)
3. Tweak rwm, steepness, thresholds
4. Try 4 transitions in state 1 too
5. Try replacing one random transition with floor-based
6. Try state 1 with camera sees cyan (state 0 robots) instead of magenta

**Exploration ideas:**
- 3-state machines
- GoToColor blue variant
- Different neighbor conditions

**LED: ONLY 0, 4, 5, 6**

## Reasoning

**Champion (score -13.76) - no improvement this round.**

The champion structure is solid. Let me think more carefully about what's happening:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=22), triggers GoAwayGreen on: random(0.9), random(0.45), neighbors>9, WHITE floor(0.95)
- State 1: GoAwayColor(green), returns to explore on: random(0.97), BLACK floor(0.75), sees magenta(0.85)

The WHITE floor condition at p=0.95 is critical - when at nest, immediately go toward blue source. The BLACK floor condition returns from source to explore.

**What to try now:**
1. The champion has been stuck at -13.76. Need more exploration.
2. Try replacing the random transition in state 1 with something more purposeful - e.g., sees cyan (own LED=6, other robots in state 0) 
3. Try `p0x3 = 1.0` (always go to GoAwayGreen when on white floor)
4. Try `p1x1 = 0.9` (more reliably return when at black floor)
5. Try different `w0x2` steepness values (8, 10, 16, 20)
6. Try 3-state machines with explicit return navigation
7. Try replacing neighbor condition with sees-color condition
8. Try removing the neighbor condition entirely (only 3 transitions in state 0)
9. Try GoToColor(blue=3) for the source-seeking state

**Key insight I haven't tried:** What if state 1 (GoAwayGreen) also transitions to GoAwayGreen when on WHITE floor? That's a self-loop issue. Or: add a 4th condition to state 1.

**Also:** The magenta camera check in state 1 sees other robots also in GoAwayGreen state (same LED=5). When they cluster near green walls, robots detect each other and return to explore. This might be helping spread robots.

Let me try:
- p0x3=1.0 (always switch to GoAwayGreen at white/nest)
- p1x1=0.85, 0.90 (stronger black floor return)
- Different steepness: 8, 10, 16
- 3-state with GoToColor(red=1) for nest navigation - wait, clr=1 is red
- Try GoAwayColor(green) + GoToColor(red) 3-state
- Try completely different topology: GoToColor(blue) directly

**LED values: ONLY 0, 4, 5, 6**
**clr values: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan**

## Reasoning

The champion has been stuck at -13.76 for 3 rounds. I need to think more carefully about escaping this local optimum.

**Deep analysis of the champion:**
- State 0 (Explore, cyan LED=6): Switches to GoAwayGreen on white floor (0.95), randomly (0.9, 0.45), or with many neighbors
- State 1 (GoAwayGreen, magenta LED=5): Returns to explore on black floor (0.75), randomly (0.97), or sees magenta (0.85)

The camera condition `sees magenta (clr=5)` in state 1 detects other robots also in state 1. The cyan LED (clr=6) of state 0 robots could be detected too.

**What I haven't tried:**
1. Replacing the neighbor-above condition (C=3) with neighbor-BELOW (C=4) - fewer neighbors triggers GoAwayGreen (dispersal signal)
2. Using gray floor detection (C=1) as a transition
3. Seeing cyan (clr=6, state 0 robots) as a camera condition in state 1
4. Having state 1 also check white floor to stay in GoAwayGreen
5. Completely different 2-state: GoToColor(blue) ↔ GoToColor(red)
6. 3-state with proper nest-return behavior using GoToColor(red=1)
7. Adjusting the random probability in state 1 significantly lower (0.5-0.7) to spend more time going away from green
8. Try condition C=4 (neighbors BELOW threshold) to trigger GoAwayGreen when isolated

**Critical realization:** The random 0.97 in state 1 means robots almost always return to explore after 1 step! That's very fast switching. What if we lower this to 0.3-0.5 to let GoAwayGreen run longer?

**LED: ONLY 0, 4, 5, 6. clr: 1=red,2=green,3=blue,4=yellow,5=magenta,6=cyan**

Let me try:
1. Champion exact copy
2. Lower p1x0 (random return) to 0.5 - spend more time in GoAwayGreen
3. Lower p1x0 to 0.3
4. Replace neighbor-above with neighbor-below in state 0
5. Replace neighbor condition with sees-cyan condition
6. Add gray floor condition
7. 3-state: Explore → GoAwayGreen → GoToRed(nest)
8. Try GoToColor(blue) in state 1 instead
9. Different steepness w=20
10. Try p0x0=1.0 (always immediately go to GoAwayGreen - but that makes explore useless)
11. Remove one random transition from state 0
12. Completely different topology
13. 4-state forager

## Reasoning

The champion has been stuck at -13.76 for 4 rounds. I need to think more carefully.

**Deep reflection on the champion behavior:**
- State 0 (Explore): Triggers GoAwayGreen when on WHITE floor (0.95) - robots at nest immediately start going toward blue source
- State 1 (GoAwayGreen): Returns to explore when on BLACK floor (0.75) - robots at source return
- The random 0.97 in state 1 means GoAwayGreen runs for about 1/(1-0.97×(1-0.75)) ≈ complex steps, but since 0.97 fires with prob 0.97 each step, expected time in state 1 = 1/(1-0.97+0.97×0.75) ≈ complicated. Actually each step: prob of leaving = 1 - (1-0.97)(1-0.75)(1-0.85) ≈ very high. Wait no - multiple conditions fire independently.

Actually the conditions are checked with OR logic: if ANY condition fires, transition. So prob of staying in state 1 = prob NONE fire = (1-0.97)(1-0.75)(1-0.85) = 0.03×0.25×0.15 = 0.001125. So robots leave state 1 almost every step! They barely do any GoAwayGreen.

**This is the key insight I've been missing!** The robot oscillates extremely rapidly. The effective behavior is a weighted mixture of exploration and GoAwayGreen with exploration dominating.

**So the real behavior:** mostly exploration with brief GoAwayGreen bursts, triggered to GoAwayGreen at nest (white floor). This creates a biased random walk away from green walls.

**To improve:** Either:
1. Make state 1 more persistent (lower random prob significantly, like 0.1 or remove it)
2. Make state 1 only exit on meaningful conditions (black floor, not random)
3. Try state 1 with only 1-2 conditions, not 3

**New ideas:**
- State 1 with only floor conditions: exit on BLACK (at source) or GRAY (between)
- Remove the random 0.97 from state 1 entirely, keep only floor and camera
- Try state 1 with p1x0=0.01 (very low random)
- Try 1-transition state 1: only exit on black floor

Let me also try completely different structures since we're stuck.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Final round - must escape -13.76 plateau.**

**Critical correction:** The errors show that condition C=3 (neighbors above threshold) needs `--p` as INTEGER and `--w` for steepness. I've been doing this correctly in the champion. The error in round 9 was `--c0x1 3 --p0x2 9 --w0x2 12.0` - I accidentally put the p/w on x2 instead of x1. Must be careful.

**Key insight from my analysis:** The champion's state 1 has prob of leaving ≈ 1 - (1-0.97)(1-0.75)(1-0.85) ≈ 0.99888 per step. So robots spend ~1 step in GoAwayGreen before returning. This is essentially: "at nest (white floor), take one step away from green, then explore again." This creates a biased walk.

**What might improve things:**
1. Make state 1 more persistent - try with only the BLACK floor exit (prob 0.85-0.95), remove randoms from state 1
2. Try GoToColor(blue=3) which actively seeks blue walls
3. Try adding a GRAY floor condition to state 0 (when on gray = in between, go to GoAwayGreen)
4. Try different combinations of floor conditions
5. Try state 1 with only 1 transition (black floor only) - forces robot to traverse all the way to source

**For this last round, focus on:**
- Champion (exact)
- State 1 with only floor-based exits (persistent GoAwayGreen)
- State 0 also triggering on gray floor
- GoToColor(blue) variant
- 3-state variants
- Completely fresh topologies

**LED: ONLY 0, 4, 5, 6. clr: 1=red,2=green,3=blue,4=yellow,5=magenta,6=cyan**
**C=3 needs INTEGER --p and REAL --w on SAME index**

Let me write 12 careful machines:

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
